<!--
The architectural exploration we’re going to embark on together is based on the Codecademy Go app as it’s written today, though the data fetching and displaying problems are likely things you’ve already worked with in your own applications. They’re general to React web applications overall. You should be able to get in a good learning or two even if you never touch React Native or Expo.
Speaking of which: we should note that I only inherited this architecture and am a React Native and Expo novice myself. This entire talk and I are therefore complete shams. You’ve been warned.

<!--
Suppose our app is meant to show three pieces of data in that home screen:
User Profile Data: a single headshot photo the user has separately uploaded in some kind of app settings.
Achievements Count: the total number of points they’ve been granted for completing various app activities.
Daily Content: some kind of ever-changing live feed of things they can do next in the app..
-->

<!--
The three pieces of data we’ll need to show in the app correspond to three common frequencies of data update frequencies. For the sake of this talk I’ll refer to these hypotheticals on a temperature spectrum:
Cold data rarely changes, and almost always due to some known queue, so it’s almost always the same each application start. User headshot photos are a great example of this: they’re only changed by the user at a fixed time, and then only rarely.
Warm data changes once in a while, commonly from known user actions. Achievements might be unlocked infrequently during the app from user behavior. 
Hot data: change this as frequently as the user asks for it! This might not change every time the user visits the app, but it should certainly update at least every day, and then after the user takes actions on it.
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 250 }
---

<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:130.37px;left:120px;top:204.81px;width:720px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:35px;font-weight:700;line-height:63px;text-align:center;position:relative;top:7px;margin-bottom:-7.09px"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:35px;font-weight:700;letter-spacing:0.7px">2. </span><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:35px;font-weight:700;letter-spacing:0.7px">Typical Approaches</span></p></div>
</div>

-->

<!--
If you haven’t played with Redux or thunks before and were hoping this talk would explain it… well, you’re partially in luck! I won’t go into how the full system works but we’ll have enough context clues to get by.
In this case, the real takeaway is that there are two parts to the logic for this hydrateData method:
First, we await a Promise.all of three data loading methods. Promise.all waits until all Promises have finished, then returns an array of their results in the provided order.
Next, we “dispatch” an “action” indicating we’ve loaded those three pieces of data. Elsewhere in the app some Redux logic we’d have to write (but not shown in the slides, for brevity) will store the data in our application state.
The darker lines show boilerplate code setting up the method as a Redux thunks: a common addon for Redux that allows dispatched actions to trigger some asynchronous code logic. This hydrateData function, when used with Redux thunks, can therefore be dispatched by views or other thunks.
-->

transition: { name: fade, duration: 250 }

<!--
If you haven’t played with React hooks before and were hoping this talk would avoid or explain them… well, you’re partially in luck (again)! I won’t go into how the full Hooks system works but we’ll have enough context clues to get by.
In this case, the Naïve component has a few pieces of relevant logic, in order:
The first time it is rendered, it dispatches the hydrateData thunk function we saw in the last slide to load data.
useEffect takes in a function and an optional array; when that array is empty like this one, the useEffect is roughly similar to componentDidMount.
Next, it selects the cold, warm, and hot data using a selector (again, not pictured here for brevity). If that data hasn’t yet been loaded, it renders loading text.
Finally, for when the component renders after the data finished loading, we render the components for each part of the page (not pictured here).
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 250 }
---

<!--
I like calling this the “naïve” approach because it’s simple and works just fine most of the time, but doesn’t do anything particularly fancy. Most of the intellectual grappling we needed for the two code examples were based on Redux and React principles around thunks and hooks.
The dark side of our naivety is the brittleness of the code. We’ll have to wait to render anything useful at all until all possible data we’d want loads. Furthermore, if a single one of the data fetching requests cannot succeed, such as from a server error or network oddity, the entire page remains in the loading state.
-->

transition: { name: fade, duration: 250 }
---

Now that the individual parts of the data visualization have individual loading behaviors, we’d likely no longer include “is it loaded?” checks in the root component. It’ll still kick off the data hydration thunk on mount, but the individual components will handle their own rendering logics.
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 250 }
---

<!--
Those individual components’ logics, therefore, takes on the burden of rendering a loading screen when their data isn’t ready. In the case of the cold data (the profile photo), if it doesn’t yet exist, we’ll show the loading placeholder. Once selectCold returns a loaded piece of data, the Cold component will rerender to show the profile photo.
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 250 }
---

<!--
Similarly, our Warm component might default its count to a “dot dot dot” loading placeholder when it doesn’t yet exist.
By the way, you might not yet be familiar with the “??” ‘question mark question mark] piece of syntax. If so, hooray, wowee, it’s probably the most exciting part of this talk for you! It’s called “nullish coalescing” and it behaves similar to “||” (pipe pipe) “or” operator, but only considers null and void to be falsy. That lets us still show a warm data value of 0 once it exists.
-->

Bold';font-size:24px;line-height:25.92px;margin-top:10px;position:relative;top:1.81px;color:#C8D7FA;list-style-type:'• ';margin-left:45.37px;padding-left:26.63px"><span style="color:#B3C6E7;font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:24px;font-weight:400">Single requests don’t delay or fail the entire pipeline</span></li></ul><p style="padding-left:40.5px;text-indent:-40.5px;font-size:24px;line-height:25.92px;margin-top:10px"><br></p><p style="font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:35px;line-height:37.8px;margin-top:10px;position:relative;top:1.88px"><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:35px;font-weight:400">Cons</span></p><ul><li style="font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:24px;line-height:25.92px;margin-top:10px;position:relative;top:1.81px;color:#C8D7FA;list-style-type:'• ';margin-left:45.37px;padding-left:26.63px"><span style="color:#B3C6E7;font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:24px;font-weight:400">Individually crafted loading visuals</span></li><li style="font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:24px;line-height:25.92px;margin-top:10px;position:relative;top:1.81px;color:#C8D7FA;list-style-type:'• ';margin-left:45.37px;padding-left:26.63px"><span style="color:#B3C6E7;font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:24px;font-weight:400">Waiting for cold data seems wasteful</span></li></ul></div>
</div>

<!--
On the surface, selective laziness seems like a great step forward from naïve laziness. The page’s loading animation makes the page feel like it’s closer to working much sooner (although, in hindsight, a half-naive strategy could have been to still have individual components render their own loading for the original all-at-once naïve loader…). Selective laziness does have a key behavioral improvement in that single requests taking a while won’t slow down the whole rendering pipeline. We render page components as soon as they’re ready. One data request failing won’t prevent others from rendering.
On the other hand, selective laziness is a little bit more code to write. We had to add in individual loading behaviors for each part of the app. That wasn’t so much code in our sample app, but honestly, if you’re in a hurry, there’s no shame in going with the simpler loading behavior. I want to be very clear here (and will reiterate towards the end of the talk) that each strategy has its own pros and cons – there’s no perfect solution to loading behavior. 
Anyway, when you think about the typical shape of data in most apps, things like profile photos really don’t change all that frequency. It seems wasteful that we’d need to always request for them when we know they probably didn’t update since last time… can we do better?
-->

layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 250 }
---

<!--
Two pieces of data in particular have already been called out as not updating particular frequently: our cold profile photo and warm achievements count. A user visiting this page might feel like they’re constantly reliving the same loading misery over and over again, with no change each time. What a predicament! If only we could persist the data locally between sessions…
https://giphy.com/gifs/bill-murray-groundhog-day-well-its-again-3o7WIQ4FARJdpmUni8
-->

<!--
We can now modify our strategy to manually keep our cold and warm data persisted in device storage. Data hydration will now involve restoring any cached data when first run, then storing newly fetched data in storage when received.
-->

layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 250 }

<!--
Here’s an implementation of that storage-first logic. I know, there are a lot of curlies and parenthesis and squigglies and whatnot here. It’s a lot of gibberish…
…but if you close your eyes, wait a second, open them again, and then really squint at the code, there’s a kind of repeatable elegance to it. It’s doing exactly what we need, asynchronously, in the order we need it to. Nifty.
Corresponding to our description’s major points, we have two groups of Promises awaited in unison:
The first Promise.all group contains two calls to retrieve data from storage; each dispatches a data hydration event when done.
The second is for the same data fetchers as our previous strategies, but with the addition of adding results to storage for the cold and warm fetchers.
https://giphy.com/gifs/bill-murray-groundhog-day-blah-XZydA31Ndgyas
-->

<!--
In summary, this manual storage methodology gives us a rather pleasant speed boost on repeat visits to our home screen. Data is available almost immediately upon visiting, and still gets checked for updates as quickly as before.
The major downside here is in the size and maintainability of code. Our hydrateData thunk has practically exploded in size! We wouldn’t want to repeat that copypasta for every piece of potentially cacheable data in the app.
-->

<!--
Of course, there is! Joy! Redux-persist is a library for Redux that does exactly what we need: it synchronizes Redux store data to your device storage. It’s compatible both with synchronous storage, such as browsers’ localStorage, and asynchronous storage, such as React Native’s async-storage.
-->

<!--
Using redux-persist to guarantee data persistence, our data flow becomes beautifully simple again! All that fancy logic we had to implement in the manual storage persistence is done for us.
-->

<!--
Summarizing our advancement in state management here: we’ve achieved similar performance gains to the manual storage strategy with much less hand-written code in our application logic. That’s a big potential benefit and something we should feel good about!
The startup performance penalty is very teeny and likely not a huge blow to your perceived application performance… but a much larger drawback does exist: when storage is always automated for you, it’s easy to accidentally display old persisted data that should have been updated. We did that in our app: hot data is shown immediately even though we only wanted the immediate visibility for cold and warm!
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 250 }
---

<!--
In other words, persistent data isn’t always “persistent”. It might not last forever, and your app will need to know how to handle that.
A few solutions exist for this. You can use the configuration options for redux-persist to set up lists of keys it should or should not persist. Its options also allow for “filter” functions; community solutions exist using those. Those work well but require an oftentimes impossible to achieve understanding of the shape of your data at the configuration layer (and aren’t particularly exciting for the purposes of this talk)…
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 250 }
---

<!--
Instead, a data-first strategy would be to store a timestamp alongside data not meant to be persisted forever. Your components can choose not to render data if the timestamp is too old. Here, that’d be just the hot data and component.
-->

<!--
At this point, we’ve achieved what is likely the maximum reasonable performance we can squeeze out of our Redux application. Data is persistent and available as soon as reasonable possible on load, but won’t be displayed if it’s too out-of-date. The architecture required some extra fine-tuning -and could easily fall back into displaying out-of-date data in a future code change- but overall it’s working pretty snazzily.
I’m satisfied with the five options displayed so far and do not have the strength of will or remaining talk time to display more.
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 250 }
---

<!--
This handy table might help you visualize where each strategy stands as applied to the very specific dummy application presented in the talk. Sure, resiliency and speed increase as the strategies get more advanced… but cleanliness and safety generally decrease.
You might be tempted to create a cutesy generalized scoring system for the rows, perhaps calculated as the total number of happy green checks subtracted by the total number of unhappy red Xs.... But that would assume an equal weighting of all factors, which really isn’t realistic for many apps. A project where ease of development and visual safety far outweigh initial or repeat performance would seemingly often choose the naïve laziness approach.
-->

<!--
As a rule of thumb for fancy data persistence, I’ve found it’s generally better to be safe than sorry. Know when you would (or wouldn’t!) care to expend effort persisting data. If you do want to persist, understand your data’s behavior and adjust your strategy accordingly
As a part of your strategy, make sure you know when to clear your persisted data altogether! User who log out of an app probably would not want the next user to receive their cached data. It’s only a matter of time before someone on a shared device loses their identity over a misuse of redux-persist.
https://cdn.britannica.com/47/188747-050-1D34E743/Bill-Gates-2011.jpg
-->

<!--
At a systemic level, the integrations seen here are only feasible because all the libraries are developed so in the open. Sure, React and Redux are separate projects – but react-redux  is able to make them work great together in because of all the open development and communication in the ecosystem. Same too with redux-persist working on top of Redux, despite having separate development cycles from separate maintainer pools.
On the flip side of the stack, React itself is open and extensible enough that a large investment such as React Native is able to work well with it and garner community projects such as @react-native-community/async-storage. Expo, which itself is open source, fits in nicely as a development tool using the openness of the ecosystem.
You get the picture.
-->

<!--
Even excluding the communal benefits, libraries opening themselves up on GitHub give themselves many invaluable advantages for transparency, approachability, and contributions.
Open source libraries can be inspected, scrutinized, even truly understood (when reasonable) by their consumers. Those contributors can empower themselves to debug through the libraries as needed to discover how they work. They say two minds are better than one: imagine having an army of users validating, reporting issues against, and sending pull requests to improve your piece of software. Magic.
https://emojipedia-us.s3.dualstack.us-west-1.amazonaws.com/thumbs/240/microsoft/209/brain_1f9e0.png
-->

<!--
The open source ecosystem isn’t perfect by any means. There are still many unsolved problems around (to name just a few): maintainer burnout, sustainable funding, community stewardship, and the inevitable politicizing of freely available software.
That being said, open source is powerful enough that we can create standardized approaches to common development problems by linking together prebuilt open source tools with bits and pieces of application logic. The next time you’re architecting a new application or feature, try not to think of it in terms of what you can build on your own. Think of how you can use or contribute to existing systems, minimizing work on your end and benefitting developers worldwide.
-->
